INDIAN AGRICULTURAL RESEARCH INSTITUTE 
(Af.LOTMENT OF RESIDENCES) REVISED RULES, 1977 

S.O. 2125. — In puisuiinec of lule 45 of the Fundamental 
Rules, Ihc President hereby makes the following rules fur- 
ther to amend the Supplementary Rules, issued the Govem- 
fnent of India, late Finance Department, letter No. 104- 
C.S.R., dated 4th February, 1922, namely ; — 

In part VIII of the said rules, for Division XXVl-J and 
the heading and rules thereunder, the following Division, 
heading and rules shall be substituted namely : — 

“Division XXVI-J-The Indian Agricultural Research Ins- 
titute (Allotment of Residences) Rules, 1977 — 

S. R. 317-1-1 : Short title, application and commence- 
ment : — 

(1) Tliese rules may be called the Indian Agricultural 
Research Institute (Allotment of Residences) Rules, 1977. 

(2) Thev shall appiv to the residential buildings belong- 
ing to Ihc Government and located within the premises of 
tlie Indian ABiicultural Research Institute. 

(3) They shall come into force on the date of their 
publication in the Official Oa/xtle. 

S. R, .317-1-2 ; Definitions : — In these rules, unles.s the 
context otherwise requires : — 

(a) ‘allotment’ means the grant of a licence to occupy 
a residence in accordance with the provisions of these rules; 

(b) ‘allotment year’ means the year beginning on 1st lan- 
uary or such or other period as may be notified by the 
Director, 

(c) ‘Direclot’ means the Diredor of the Institute and 
includes any other officer authorised by him to act on his 
behalf; 

(d) ‘eligible office’ means the office of the Indian Agri- 
cultural Research Institute and its sub-stations, the staff of 
which has been declared by the Director as eligible for acco- 
mmodation undei these rules; 

(e) ‘emoluments' means the emoluments as defined in 
rule 45-C of the Fundamental Rules, but excluding the com- 
pensatory allowance. 

Explanation . -In the case of an officer who is under 
suspension the emoluments drawn by him on the first day 
of the allotment year in which he is placed under suspen- 
MOn, or if he is placed under suspension on the first day of 
the allotment year, the emoluments drawn by him immedia- 
tely before (hat date shall be taken as emoluments; 

(f) ’fanidy’ means the wife or husband, as the case may 
be, and children, step-children, legally adopted children, par- 
ents. brothers or sisters as ordinaiily reside with, and me 
dependent on, the officer;

(g) ‘Government’ means the Central Govcmnienf; 

(h) ‘Institute’ means the Indian Agricultural Research 
Institute, New Delhi; 

(i) ‘licence fee' means the sum of money payable monthly 
in accoidance with (he provisions of the Eundamcntal Rules 
m respect of a residence allotted under these rules; 

(Jl ‘piiority date’ of an officer for aUotmenl of accommo- 
dation under these rules means the earliest date of holding 
continuous qualifying appointment on a post under the Ins-

Provided that the eligibility for the type of residence under 
ihe provision of S R. 3I7-T-5 shall be determined on the

be specified: 

Prosided further the officers eligible to Type V or Type 
VI shall also be eligible to allotment of residences in type 
next below to their eligible type, namely. Type XV and Type 
V respectively: 

Provided also that where the priority date of two or more 
officers is Ihe same seniority amongst themselves shall bo 
determined by ihe amount of emoluments, the officer in 
receipt of higher emoluments taking procedure over the 
officet in receipt of lower emoluments; and where the emo- 
luments are equal, by the length of service; 

(k) ‘residence’ means any residence for the time being 
under the admini.stralivc control of the Director; 

(l) ‘.subletting’ includes sharing of accommodation by an 
allottee with another person with or without payment of 
licence fee by such other person. 

‘Explanation’ Any sharing of accommodation be an allo- 
ttee with close relations shall not be deemed to be sub- 
letting; 

(ni) ‘transfer’ means a transfer from an office of the 
Institute at New Delhi to any other office of the Institute 
oulside New Delhi or vice versa and also deputation to a 
post in an ineligible office or organisation; 

(n) ‘temporary transfer’ means a transfer which involves 
an absence foi a pciiod of not exceeding four months; 

(o) Type’ in relation to an Officer means the type of rcsi 
dcnce to which he shall be eligible under S,R. 317-J-5, 

S.R. 317-J-3 Ineligibility of officers owning houses for 
allotment under these rules : — 

No officer shall be eligible for allotment of a residence 
under these rules if he /she owns a house in full or in part in 
his/her own name or in the nanie of his/her wife or hus- 
band or any dependent within the municipal limits of New 
Delhi/Dcihi. 

S. R. 317-J-4 Allotment to husband and wife. Eligibility 
in cnve.s ol officers who are married to each other ; — 

il)Wheie both husband and wnfe are employed under the 
Institute, the title of each of the two officers to allotment 
of a residence under these rules shall be considered inde- 
pendently, 

(2) No officer shall be allotted a lesidcnce under these rules 
if the wife or the husband, as the case may be, of the officer 
has already been allotted a residence, unless such residence 
is surrendered : 

Provided that I his sub-rule shall not apply where the hus- 
band and wife arc rc.sidiiig separately in pursuance of an 
order of judicial separation made by any court. 

(3) Where two officers in occupation of separate resi- 
dences allotted under these jules marry each other, they shall, 
within one month of the marriage surrender one of the resi- 
dences 

(4) If a residence is not surrendered, as required by sub- 
rule (2), the allotment of the residence of the lower Typo 
shall be deemed to have been cancelled, on Ihe expiry of 
such period and if the residences are of the same Type, 
the allotment of such one of them as the Director may de- 
cide shall be deemed to have been cancelled on ihc ex- 
pin of such period.

S.R. 317-J-13 Surrender of an allotment and pciiod of 
notice 

(1) An officer may at any time surrender an allotment by 
giving intimation so as to reach the Director at least ten days 
before the date of vacation of the residence. Phe aflotmcnt 
of the lesidence shall be deemed to be cancelled with effect 
from the eleventh day after the day on which the letter is 
received by the Director, or the date specified in the letter, 
whichever is later. If he fails to give due notice, he shall 
be responsible for payment of licence fee for ten days oi the 
number of days by which the notice given by him falls short 
of ten days, provided that the Director may accept a notice 
for a short period. 

(2) An officer who surrendcis the residence undci sub- 
rule (1) .shall not be considered again for allotment of accom- 
modation at the Institute for a period of one year fjom the 
date of such surrender . 

S.R. 317-J-14 Change of residence : — - 

f 1 ) An officer to whom a residence has been allotted under 
these ndes may apply for a change to another residence of 
the same type or a residence of the type to which be is efigi- 
blc under S.R. 317-J-5 whichever is lowei, provided that not 
more than one change shall be allowed to any ‘.iich olficer in 
rcspicct of one type of residence allotted to the officer. 

(2) Air applications for change made in the form prcsciib- 
ed by the Director and received upto the 19th day of a calen- 
dar month shall be included in the waiting list in the subse- 
quent month. For the purpme of this rule, the officers whose 
names are included in the waiting list, in an earficr month shall 
be senior on block to those whose names are iucludcd in the 
list in subsequent months. The inter-se seniority of the offi- 
cers included in the list in any particular month shall be de- 
termined in the order of their priority dates. 

(3) Change shall be offered in order of seniority determined 
in accordance with sub-rule (2) and having regard to the 
officer’s preferences, as far as possible. 

(4) If an officer fails to accept a change of residence offer- 
ed to him within five days of the issue of stich offer or allot- 
ment, he shall not Ire considereed again for a change of resi- 
dence of that type. 

(5) An officer wlio, after accepting a change of residence 
fails to take possession of the same shall be charged licence 
fee for .such residence in accordance with the provisions of sub- 
rule (1) of S.R. 317-1-11 in addition to the normal licence fee 
under" F.R. 45-A for the residence already in his possession, 
the allotment of which shall continue to subsist. 

S.R. 317-J-15 Change of residence in the event of death of a 
member of the famfly : — 

Notwithstanding anything contained in S.R. 3I7-J-14, an 
officer may be allowed a change of re.sidencc on the death 
of any member of his family if he applies foi' a change within 
three months of such occuri cnce, provided that the change may 
Ire given in the same type of residence and on the same floor 
as the residence already alfottcd to the Officer. 

S. R. 317-J-16 Mutual ertchangc of residence: — 

Officers to whom residences of the same type have been 
allotted under these rules may apply for permission for mutual 
exchange of their lesidcnces. Permission of mutual exchange 
may be granted if both the officers arc leasonably expected to 
be on duty in Delhi and to reside in their mutually exchanged 
residences for at least six months from the date of approval 
of such exchange. 

S.R. 317-J-17 Transfer to non-family stations ; — 

If an officer is transferred to a station where he is not 
permitted to or advised by the Dircctoi to lake his family 
witli him and the residence allotted to him nndei these rules 
as required hy the family for the bona fide educational needs 
of his children, he may be allowed, on request, to retain 
the residence on payment of licence fee under F.R, 45-A 
till the end of the current academic session of his children 
studying in Delhi.

S.R. 317-J-18 Maintenance of residences : — 

The Officer to whom a residence has been allotted shall 
maintain the residence and premises in a clean condiffon 
to fhc satisfaction of the Director and the Municipal 
Corporation of Delhi. Such officer shall not grow any tree, 
shrubs or plants contrary to the instructions issued by the 
Director nor cut or lop off any existing tree shrubs in any 
garden, courtyard or compound attached to the residence, 
save with the prior permission in writing of the Director. 
Trees, plantation or negetation, grown and rearing of poultry 
or cattle in contravention of these niles may be caused to he 
removed at the risk and the cost of the officer concerned. 

S.R. 317-J-19 Subletting and sharing of residences ;■ — 

fl) No officci shall share the residence allotted to him 
or any of the out-honscs, garages and stables appurtenant 
thereto except with the employees of the Institute eligible 
for allotment of residences under these rules. The servants 
quarters, out-houses, garages and stables may be used only 
for the bona-fide puiposes including residence of the sci-vants 
of the allottee or for such purposes as may be permitted by 
the Director. 

(2) No officer shall sublet the whole of his residence 
provided that an officer piocceding on leave may accommo- 
date in the residence any other officer eligible to share ac- 
commodation, as a caretaker, for the period specified in S.R. 
317-1-10(2), but not exceeding six months. 

(3) Any officer who shares or sublets his residence with- 
out the prior permission of the Director shall do so at his 
own risk and responsibility and shall remain personally 
responsible for any licence fee payable in respect of the 
residence and for any damage caused to tlie residence or its 
precincts beyond fair wear and tear. 

S.R. 317-J-20 Consequences of breach of rules and condi- 
tions. 

(1) If an officer to whom a residence has been allotted 
unauthonsedly sublets the residence or charges rent from the 
sharer at a rate which the Director considers execessive oi 
erects any unauthoiised structure in any part of the residence 
or usc,s the I'csidence or any portion thereof for any purposes 
other than that for which it is meant or tempers with the 
electric or wafer connections or commit any other breach 
of these lilies or of the terras and conditions of allotment 
or uses the residence or premises or permits or suffers the 
residence or premises to be used for any other purpose 
which the Director considers to be improj^r or conducts 
himself in a manner which In his opinion is prejudicial to 
the maintenance of harmonious relation with his neighbours 
or has knowingly furnished incorrect information m any 
application of written statement with a view to securing the 
allotment, the Director may without prejudice to any other 
disciplinary action that may be taken against him, cancel the 
allotment of the residence. 

Explanation. — In this sub-rule, the expression ‘Officer’ in- 
cludes unless the context otherwise required a member of 
his family and person claiming through the Officer, 

(2) If any officer sublets a residence allotted to him or 
any portion thereof or any of the out-houses garages or 
stables appurtenant thereto, in contravention of these rules, 
he may, without prejudice to any other action that may bo 
taken against him be charged enhanced licence fee not ex- 
ceeding four times the standard licence fee under F.R, 45-A 
or market licence fee whichever is the highest. The quantum 
ol market licence fee is to be recovered and the period for 
which the same mav be recovered in each case shall be 
dciided by the Director on merits. In addition the officer 
mav be debarred from sharing the residence for a epecillcd 
period in future as may be decided by the Director. 

(3) Where action to cancel the allotment is taken on 
account of unauthorised subletting of the premises by the 
allottee, a period of 15 days shall be allowed to the ^Ottee, 
and any other person re.siding with him therein to vacate 
the premises The allotment shall be cancelled with effect 
from the date of vacation of the premises or expiry of the 
period of 15 days from the date of orders for the can- 
cellation of the allotment, whichever is earlier, 

f4) Where allotment of a residence is cancelled for conduct 
prejudicial to the maintenance of harmonious relations with 



II — ir>f 3 ( i i ) ] 'ff w ' 75 2 3 


neighbours, the officer ut the discreation of the Director 
may be allotted another reeidence in the same class at any 
other place. 

t5) The Direcloj shall be competent to take all or any 
of the actions under sub-rules (1) to (4) and also to declare 
the officer, who commits a breach of these rules and instruc- 
tions issued to him, to be ineligible for allotment of residential 
accommodation for a period not exceeding three years. 

S.R. 3I7-J-21 Overstayal in residence after cancellation of 
allotment : — 

Where, after an allotment has been cancelled 
or is deemed to be cancelled under any pro- 
vision continued in these rules, the rc.sidence 
remains or has remained in occupation of 
the officer to whom it was allotted or of any 
person claiming through his, such officer shall 
DC liable to pay damages W use and occu- 
pation of the residence, services, fuiniture 
and garden charges, equal to the market 
licence fee as may be determined by the 
Government from to time. 

Proyided that an officer, in special cases, may be 
allowed by the Director to retain a residence on 
payment of twice the standard licence fee under 
F.R. 45-A, or twice the pooled standard licence 
fee under F.R. 45-A, whichever is higher, if for 
a period not exceeding six months beyond the 
period permitted under S.R. 317-J-I0(2). 

S,R.317-J-22 Continuance of allotments made prior to the 
issue of these rules: — 

Any valid allotment of a residence which is sub- 
sisting immediately before the commencement 
of these rules under the rules then in force shall 
be deemed to be an allotment duly made under 
these rules notwithstanding that the officer to 
whom it has been made is not entitled to a resi- 
dence of that type under S.R. 317-J-5 and all 
the proceeding provisions of these rules shall 
apply in relation' to that allotment and that 
officer accordingly. 

S.R.31 7-3-23 Interpretation of rules; — 

If any question arisc.s as to the interpretation of 
the rules, it shall be decided by the Director. 

S,R,3 17-3-24 Relaxation of rules: — 

The Government may, for reason to be recor- 
ded in writing, relax all or any of the provisions 
of these rules in the case of any officer or residence 
or class of officers or type of residences. 

S.R. 317-3-25 Delegation of powers or functions; — 

The Government may delegate any or all the 
powers conferred upon it by these rules to any 
officer under its control, subject to such condi- 
tions as it may deem fit to impose.

the Central Government hereby constitutes an Industrial Tri-

S.O. 2130. — In pur.suancc of .section 17 of the Industiial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Cential Uos eminent 
hereby publishes the following award of the Central Govern- 
ment Industrial Tribunal, .laipur in the industrial ilispulc 
between the employers in relation to the management of the 
State Bank of India, laipur and their workmen, \thich was 
received by the Ccntial Government on 7-6-77. 

CENTRAL INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL NO. 1, 
RAJASTHAN, JAIPUR 
Case No, CIT.2/76 

Ref ; — Government of India, Mlnistiy of Labour, Noti- 
fication No. L. 12012/141 /75/D If/A dated the 
21st January, 1976. 

In the mutter of an Industrial Dispute 

BETWEEN 
Shii Gopal Singh 
AND 

State Bank of India, Jaipui 
APPEARANCES ; 

Shri Ashok Parihar — ^For the Workman, 

Shri S. K. Sharma — For the Bunk. 

Date of Award ; 24th May, 1977 
AWARD 

By its notification dated 21-1-76, the Central Government 
has referred the following industrial dispiilc to this Tribunal 
for adjudication. 

“Whether the action of the State Bank of India, Sanga- 
neri Gate Branch, Jaipur m terminating the sciviccs 
of Shri Gopal Singh, Godown-Darban. with ellect 
from the 1st August, 1974 is justified and legal “ 
If not to what relief is the said workman entitled 7“ 

The dispute is between the State Bank of India and its 
cx-employee Shri Gopal Singh, hereinaftci to be referred as 
the workman. 

The case set up by the workman in his statement of claim 
is that he was appointed by the State Bank of India, Branch 
Jaipur, as a Godown-Darban on 8-12-70. Since then, he 
was working in that capacity with no complaint against him. 
The appointment was of permanent riature. He continued 
to work as Godown-Darban upto 31-7-74. When he re- 
orted himself for duty on 1-8-74, he was not taken on duty 
y the Bank Manager. He was informed that his ser\tces 
stood terminated with effect from that day. The workman 
approached the Bank Authoiities to redress his grievances of 
the tcrim'nation of his services, but his attempt proved abor- 
tive, He sent a notice lo lire Bank raising an industrial dis- 
pute about his termination. But, this notice also remained 
tinreplted. Tlie maltcr was then taken to tire Rcpional La- 
bour Commissioner, Central, fui conciliation. Rut, Ihcrc 


also no conciliation could take place due to tire non-coope- 
rative attitude of the Bank. Lhe Conciliatron Officer sub- 
mitted hLs failure report to the Government of India. The 
Government of India then made the present reference. It 
was alleged that the termination of the workman’s services 
was bad. No rnqurry was held against him. Even if it is 
taken to be a case of retienchment, it was not valid, becau^ 
the mandatory provisions of S. 25-E of the Industrial Dis- 
putes Act, 1947, hereinafter to be referred as the Act, were 
not complied with. The relief claimed is that he be re- 
instated with full back wages. 

The claim of the workman was resisted by the Bank. 
Jn its written statement, the Bank admitted that the work- 
man was appointed as Godown Darban on 8-12-70 and 
his appointment ended on 31-7-74. The .appointment was 
purely temporary. The workman worked intejinilently. The 
Bank was left with no temporary post of Godown-Darban 
m August, 1974. The workman was, therefore, oflered to 
work as Messanger. The workman worked as Messenger 
for one day on 1-8-74. Thereafter, he did not turn up to 
work. He thus voluntarily abondonod the services of the 
Bank. The workkman, after some time, apptoached the Bank 
for le-employmcnt. He w,ts given the employment of a 
Messenger on 18-11-75 and since then he was woiking in 
that capacity at Sikar Branch. It was alleged that no indus- 
t.i.'d dispute existed between the parties, and ns such the 
icfcrcnce was not maintainable. 

Both the parties have led ojal and documentary evidence, 
'lhe workman examined IiimscJf, while in icbuttal the Bank 
examined its Manager Shri K. K. Bhaigava. 

1 shall first take up the prelimiiiaiy objection of the flank 
that the reference is not maintainable, as no industiial dis- 
pute existed between the parties, 1 find no merit in this 
contention. The workman complains that his removal from 
service, which in essence is retrenchment, was illegal. Such 
a dispute must be taken to be an industrial dispute between 
an employer and employee, Theic being no force in the 
preliminary objection of the Bank, it is hereby repelled. 

There is not much dispute as logards the facts involved 
in the case. Undisputedly, the workman, Shri Gopal Singh, 
was appointed Godown-Darban by the Bank on 8-12-70 and 
he worked in that capacity upto 31-7-74. The Bank has 
submitted a statement showing the details of the services of 
the workman from 8-12-70 to 31-7-74. He remained in 
employment upto 31-7-74. The chart further shows that 
he worked from 1-1-73 to 31-7-74 with no bieak during this 
period. Thus, he had completed one year's continuous ser- 
vice as defined in S. 25-B of the Act. The chart further 
shows that from 8-12-70 to 31-7-74, the workman worked 
for 1334 days in all. 

The workman, Shri Gopal Singh, staled that he worked 
as Godown-Darban from 8-12'-70 to 31-7-74, He was 
cross-examined, but with no result. The Bank also exami- 
ned its manager, Shri K. K. Bhargava, who admitted that 
the workman worked from 18-12-'70 to 31-7-74, 

Sec. 25-B defines continuous service. Its sub-sec. (2) 
lays down that a workman shall be said to be in continuous 
service for a pciiod of one year, if he, during a period of 
12 calendar months preceding the date with reference to 
which the calculation is to be made, has actually worked 
for 240 days. As stated earlier the statement filed by the 
Bank shows that the workman had worked from 1-7-73 to 
31-7-74 as Godown-Darban. He had thus worked for more 
than 240 days dining the period of 12-calcndar months pre- 
ceding 31-7-74, which is the date, when his services were 
terminated. 

The stand taken by the Bank is that it was left with no 
post of Godown-Darban. \s such, the workman's services 
were terminated and he was offered an allcrnate job of 
Messanger. The workman worked as Messanger foi cne 
day on 1-8-74. Thereafter, he voluntarily abondoned (he 
services. 

Now, if the woiknian voliintarilv abondoned the job, 
(here is no question of termination of his scriiccs, or his 
being retrenched. The workman denied that be was offered 
the job of .a Messanger or that he worked ns a Messenger 
for due d.ay on 1-8-74, or that he voluntarily left this job. 



2324 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA ; JUKE 25, 1977/ASADHA 4, 1899 


[Part II— Sec. 3(ii)] 


In view of these rival contentions, it was for the Bank to 
prove that the workman was offered the alternate job of a 
Messenger, he worked for one day as Messenger, and then 
voluntarily left the service. The Bank has adduced no 
evidence. Of course, the Bank examined .Shri K. K. Bhar- 
gava. But, his testimony on this point is far fiom being 
Messenger, he worked for one day as Messenger, and then 
posted at Jaipur Branch since 6-12-74, He further admitted 
that the job of Messenger was offered to the workman not 
by him but by his predecessor Shri H. K. Agarwal, the 
then Manager, Jaipur Branch. He further stated that this 
offer was verbal and not in writing. He also admitted that 
Shri H. K. Agarwal is alive and is in the Bank service. He 
further admitted that the offer for Messenger’s job was not 
made to the workman in his presence, An analysis of his 
testimony shows that it is entirely hearaav in nature. The 
offer of Messenger’s job was not made in his presence. He 
can, therefore, have no knowledge about these things. A 
fact cannot be proved by hearsay evidence. 

There are then some circumstances which negatived the 
stand taken by the Bank. Uie workman icrvcd a notice on 
the Bank and raised an industrial dispute therein against 
his illegal retrenchment. Shri K. K. Bhargava in cross-exa- 
mination admitted the receipt of notice on 30-10-74. He 
further admitted that no reply of this notice was sent to 
the workman by the Bank. Tf the alternate Job of Messan- 
ger had been offered to the workman by the Bank, the Bank 
could have never remained silent. It would have never 
left the workman’s notice unanswered The very fact that 
the Bank sent no reply to the workman’s notice raised strong 
inference that its plea of offering alternate Job of Messenger 
to the workman has not an iota of truth. This plea is an 
after thought. 

Admittedly, the workman has been reemployed by the 
Bank as Messenger on 18-11-75 at Sikar Branch. Had the 
offer of this Job been made to the workman on 1-8-74, he 
woidd have accepted it as he has accepted on 18-11*75. 
This fact also speaks volumes against the Bank. There fa 
thus no substance in the Bank's plea that it offered the job 
of Messenger to the workman on 1-8-74 and he refu-sed it. 

I have held above that the workman worked continuorrsly 
for one year as defined in S- 25-B of the Act. Sec. 25-F 
of the Act lays down the conditions precedent to retrench- 
ment of workman. The provisions of S, 25-F make it amply 
clear that no workman shall be retrenched until ; (1) he 
has been given one month’s notice in writing indicating the 
reasons for retrenchment or wages in lieu of such notice, (2) 
payment of compensation equivalent to 15 days average pay 
for every completed year and continuous service or any part 
thereof in excess of six months ; and (3) notice to the 
appropriate Government in the prescribed manner. 

In the instant case, the workman was not given one 
month’s notice in writing or_tho wages in lieu of such notice. 
He was also not paid the compensation equivalent to 15 
days average pay for every completed year of continuous 
service or any part thereof in excess of six months. Any 
retrenchment, which contravenes the provisions of S 25-F 
is illegal and cannot be given effect to The provisions of 
S, 25-F were recently considered by the Hon’blc Supreme 
Court in AIR 1976 S. C. 1111 and AIR 1977 S.C. 31. It 
was laid down in these authorities that when the retrench- 
ment is in contravention of Sec. 25-F. it is illegal. It was 
further held in these authorities that when the retrenchment 
is illegal, the relief available to the workman, who was 
victim of the Illegal retrenchment, is his reinstatement with 
full back wages. The retrenchment of the workman, Shri 
Gopal Singh, is illegal, because his retrenchment was In 
direct violation and contravention of the mandatory provi- 
sions of S. 25-F of the Act, 

Learned representative for the Bank contended vehemently 
that back wages should not be allowed to the workman. It 
was argued that the workman did not adduce evidence to 
show that he remained unemployed since 1-8-74 to 17-11-75 
when he was given fresh appointment bv the Bank, I find 
no force in the contention. In the written statement, the 
Bank did not raise the allegation that the workman sought 
any employment from 1-8-74 to 17-11-75. The workman 
was also not cross-examined on this noint. As such, he is 
entitled to get full back wages from 1-8-74 *0 17-11-75 and 
the difference in wages from 18-11-75 till his leinstatement 
on the post of Godown-Darban, or anv other equivalent 
post. 


I. therefoio, pass the following award 

1, The retrenchment of the workman, Shri Gopal Singh 
'.vas illegal, and 

2, He vrill be forthwith re-lnstated with full back wages. 
He will get full back wages from 1-8-74 to 17-11-75 ; 

3, He will further get the difference in between the pay 
scales of Godown Darban and Messenger from 18-11-75 
till he is re-instated by the Bank. 

The award is submitted to the Central Government for 
publication. 

S. S. BY AS, ludgc, Presiding Officer

3. There are two points in dispute between the manage- 
ment of the Moonidih Project and the United Coal Workers’ 
Union, Moonidih Branch. The first dispute relates to the 
proper classification of 9 workmen and the second dispute 
IS in respect of payment of Project Allowance to all the 
workmen employed in the Moonidih Project. I propose to 
deal with these disputes in seriatim. 

4. Bindeshwari Gope, Sukhdeo Gope, .Aklu Mahato, 
Chhabinath Mahato, Kala Chand Gope, Jadu Gope, Fanlush 
Mahato, Vudhisthir Karmakar, Bhim Gorai, Sudun Mahato, 
Lattu Mahato, Deo Muni Mahato and Jagdish Mahato claimed 
(hat they were being designated and shown as General Maz- 
doors, Category-!, in the Moonidih Project and vvere being 
paid Category t wages even though they were actually per- 
forming the duties of Tyndals “Heavy” category TV and were 
entitled to category IV wages. Tb/^ cause was taiken up 
by the Secretary, United L o.il Workers, Union, Moonidm 
Branch with the Project Officer, Moonidih Project on Febru- 
ary 27, 1973 by the letter Ext, W-l. The demand received 
no satisfaction and, therefore, the Union served a strike 
notice Ext. M-1 on the Project Officer on December 24, 1973 
of their intention lo go on a strike from January 14, 1974. The 
strike notice was accompanied by a Charter of 25 demands. 
Demand No. 4 reads thus : “Tyndal Mazdoors at present are 
being paid at the rate of Category I only being designated as 
General Mazdoors. All such workmen should be given the 
due designation of Tyndal Mazdoor and be placed In Cate- 
gory V”. The A.L.C.(C) called for the comments of the 
Proiect Officer on the charter of demands. Ext. M-2 clafeH 
February 2, 1974 contain the comments of the Moonidih 
Ptojecl on these 25 demands. With regard to demand No. 4, 
the comments are as follows : “The statement is not correct. 
Person performing the Job of Tyndal has been placed in 
Category IV. The demand is vague. The Union is requested 
to submit specific names for the consideration of the manage- 
ment.” The Union was thereupon required to offer its com- 
ments on the comments of the management. The Union’s 
comments are contained in Ext. W-2 dated February 14, 
1974. The comments are “The specific cases of the concerned 
Tyndals (now kept in Category I as General Mazdoors) was 
earlier forwarded to the management vide union’s letter No. 
CWD/MND/10/73, dated 27-2-1973. For ready reference 
the names arc given below again.” Nine names were given 
and the names of Sudan Mahato, Lattu Mahato, Deo Muni 
Mahato and Jagdish Mahato were omitted because admittedly 
after the service of Ext. W-l .and before the date of the 
comments, these four had been promoted by the Project 
Orncer as Tyndals, Cat. TV, The dispute thtrs is confined 
to the remaining nine whose names are specified in item 
No. 1, of the Schedule to the reference. 

5. In addition to the above historical back-ground, we 
have the pleadings of the parties. The case of the nine work- 
men is that the thirteen, including them, had been performing 
for Quite a long time the job of handling heavy machineries, 
Cab'c drums and other heavy materials from stores to pits 
and to places of underground workings and of assisting 
Electrical and Mechanical Fitters In tbeir job of shifting, 
installing and dismantaling of machineries and materials In 
the mine, all of which are jobs of Heavy Tyndals who arc 
time-rated Cefagory TV workmen, but the management was 
treating them as General Mazdoors who are time-rated Cate- 
gory 1 workmen. They further alleged that their Union 
made a demand on Febnrary 27, 1973 for the proper classi- 
fication of these thirteen as Category IV workmen but the 
management classified foui- of them as Catecory TV workmen 
but did not accept the demand in lespect of the remaining 
nuM’- Their Union then gave a strike notice on Febniary 4, 
1974 and the charter of demands included the demand for 
their proper classification, Their prayer is that since the 


demand was rejected by the management, the Tribunal should 
accept it and direct their classification as Heavy Tynadals, 
Category IV. The management, in its written statement, has 
admiticcl the receipt of the demand letter Ext. W-l and the 
strike notice Ext. M-1 and pleaded that since the demand 
was vague, it was opposed in the conciliation proceedings 
and is opposed now. It has been further pleaded tb^ 
demand made was for classification in Category V and the 
industrial dispute laised and referred is for classification in 
Category IV and this makes it amply clear that the industrial 
dispute referred is wholly different from the industrial dispute 
raised and as such the Tribunal has no jurisdiction to decide 
the dispute that has been referred. It is then pleaded that 
these nine workmen have never worked as Heavy Tyndals/ 
Tyndal Mistries and the management is justified in refusing 
to classify them as Category IV workmen The management 
filed a rcjoindei also to the written statement of the work- 
men. In that, It is slated that four out of the thirteen were 
found lo be working as Heavy Tyndals and their demand 
was accepted and they wcie classified as Category IV work- 
men hill these nine were only working as General Mazdoors 
and were not assigned the duties of handling heavy machine- 
ries, cable drums and other heavy mnteiials from stores to 
pits and lo places of underground workings and they were 
not assisting Mechanical or Electrical Fitteis in shifting, 
installing and dismantaling machineries and other materials, 
jobs which are done by Heavy Tyndals and, therefore, their 
demand for classification as Heavy Tyndals, Category IV, Is 
unjustified. The Union also filed a rejoinder to the written 
statement of the management, in which the original pleas 
have been repeated and it has been contended that there was 
neither any vagueness about nor any difference between the 
original demand made and the industrial dispute referred and 
the Tribunal has the jurisdiction to decide the dispute. 

6. Before entering into the controversy, I would like to 
give certain facts which are not disputed, and are indeed, 
indisputable. Chapter VIII — Section-A, Vol, I of the Report 
of tne Central Wage Board for the Coal Mining Industry 
deals with timc-iatcd categories. In paragraph 15 of Chapter 
VllI — Section-A, the Board classified time-rated workmen into 
six categories. In paragraph 16, the Board mentioned that 
their recommenations for fitting existing workmen into the 
new six categories together with the nomenclatures and their 
job discriptions are contained in Appendix V to their report. 
Item No, 3 of Category I in Appendix V, relates to "Fittets”. 
It mentions that a fitter is a workman who accompwiles a 
Fitter/Electrician/Carpcnter etc. and carries tools or mate- 
rials. Admittedly, these nine workmen have been classified 
as Category I workmen under this item. Item No. 22 of 
Category II, Appendix V refers to “Fitter Helper” as a man 
who has gained some experience as a Fitter Mazdoor and 
has irnproved status by practice until he can do elemen- 
tary lifting work. He may be a mechanical or electrical 
helper aa the case may be. Item No. 5 of .Oitegoiy IV, 

Appemlix V concerns Tyndals. A Tyndal is a man generally 
employed in moving cngincerinB stores, drums of Oil and 
greases. He is also responsible for erection, dismantaling 
of structures and installation and withdrawal of machinery. 
Item No, 1 of Category V, Appendix V, deals with a Tyndal 
Supervisor. He is a man experienced in handling materials 
and in engineering structures who is incharge of a gang of 
Tyndals (Heavy). The claim of those nine workmen is for 
classification under item No. V of Category IV. Para 19 
of Chapter VUI. — Section-A gives the wages of the workmen 
belonging to the six categories. The daily minimum wage 
of a Categojy I workman is Rs. 5. There is an annual 
increment of 10 paise per day and the maximum daily wage 
rises to Rs. 6 in a .span of 10 ycais. The daily minimum 
wage of a Category TV workman is Rs. 6.90 There is an 
annual increment of 20 paise per day and the maximum 
daily wage rises to Rs. 8,90 in a duration of 10 years. The 
wages have been revised with effect from January 1, 1975 
by the Joint Bipartite Wage Negotiating Committee. The 
revised rate of Category I i.s Rs. 10 which rises to Rs. )2 
m 10 years with an annual increment of 20 raise per day. 
The revised rate of Category TV workman is Rs. 12.75 
which rises to Rs. 16.85 in 10 years with an annual incre- 
ment of 41 paise per day. 

7. I have to see whether these nine workmen are General 
Xi.i/doors (Fitters) mentioned at item No. 3 of Category I 
III Anpendix V or they are Tyndals mentioned at item No. 5 
(tt Lategoiv IV in Appendix V, This is a queriioii of fact.

trom the industrial diRpiite\ raised.

8. I will dispose oi the scoond question fiist. I do not 
think there is any vagueness or indclimteness about the dis- 
pute. In Ext. W-J, the demand was made for clMsification 
of thirteen workmen in Category IV in place of their existing 
classification in Categoty I. The raanagement accepted the 
demand and classified four out of thirteen as Category IV 
workmen. The dispute in respect of these four, therefore, 
came to an end. The strike nottce certainly did not specify 
the names of the remaining nine and that was the reason 
why the management took the stand before the A.T.C. that 
demand No. 4 in the charter of demands was vague. This, 
to my mind, was an untenable stand. The management 
should have known us to who the workman were because 
they were in possession of Ext. W-1 from February 27, 1973 
which contained the names of all the thirteen workmen. 
Again, they knew that they had promoted four and thus 
they could have worked out who the remainirrg nine were. 
In any case, the names of these nine were repeated by the 
Union m their comments Ext. W-2. There is thus no 
vagueness about the names. Likewise, I do not think that 
the dispute referred is different from the dispute raised. The 
dispute was for pioper classification of nine workmen from 
their jobs of General Mazdoors to the jobs of Tyndals 
“Heavy”. In the demand Ext. W-1, the categoiy given was 
category IV. It is only in the charter of demands that by 
inadvertence the category mentioned was Categony V and not 
Category IV. Category V, as has been seen above, refers 
to Tyndal Supervisors who is the leader of a gang of Heavy 
Tyndals. Obviously, these nine did not claim classification 
as Tyndal Supervisors but only as Heavy Tyndals which is 
covered by item No. 5 of Category TV. No party was ever 
in doubt us to the real nature of the demand; and in any 
case social justice does not favour such hair-splitting con- 
tentions. 1, therefore, bold that the Tribunal has the juris- 
diction to decide the dispute about proper classification. 

9. There is both oral and documentary evidence about the 

nature of the job being performed by these nine, and the 
truth has to be found out on the basis of this evidence. 
MW-1 K. Bagchi IS the Senior Executive Engineer, Electrical 
and Mechanical, in the Moonidih Projecl. He has deposed 
that these nine assist fitters as Fitter Mazdoors and 
they are Category I workmen and are shown as such in 
their service records from the respective dates of their initial 
appoinlmenls, He has further deposed that they do not 
handle heavy machineries, cable drums and other heavy 
materials from stores to pits or to places of underground 
workings; and they do not dismantle machineries and other 
materiais, used in the mine. He has then deposed that there 
is a separate gang of mazdoors and drifting coal face miners 
who do the job of handling heavy machineries, cable drums 
and other materials and of dismantling machineries. He 
has finally deposed that these nine are not Heavy Tyndals 
or Tyndal Mistrics. In cross-examination, he clarified the 
position by stating that fitter mazdoors, like these nine, arc 
associated with the fitters for the purpose of carrying tools, 
tightening nuts and bolts, handling wrenches and helping 
them in the maintenance of machineries and electrical ap- 
paratus, Fittcrs-helpers are different from Fitter Maz- 
doors. Filter-helpers do the same job which rue done by 
Fittei -Mazdoors, but they are more experienced in doing the 
job. He has denied that these nine work as light or heavy 
Tyndals and has stated that the job of handling heavy 
machineries is being carried on not by Ihem but % Shaft- 
Sinking Miners. As against his evidence, we have the evi- 
dence of S. N. Singh WW-1, an Electrician; Kalachand Gope 
WW-2, one of the concerned workmen; and Shridhar Chandra 
Roy WW-3, Electrical Chargeman. S. N, Singh has deposed 
that these nine do the job of transporting gear boxes of 
conveyor motors, heavy pumps, cables and other heavy 

materials. In cross-examination, he has said that there are 
two separate departments, namely, Electrical Engineering 
Department and Mechanical Engineering Depaitment. He 
has then stated that these nine work in the Electrical 
Engineering Department. T do not think that he has given 
a coi rcct statement that these nine work in the Electrical 
Engineering Department. He has admitted that gear boxes 
and conveyor pans arc required by Ihe Mechanical Engineer- 
ing Department; and if that be so, these nine, assuming that 
they cariv gear boxes and conveyor pans, would be working 
in the Mechanical Engineering Department and not in the 
Eleclrical Engineering Department, The witness is the S-'crc- 
tary of the Union and had sponsored the cause of these 
nine. He had also taken active part in the conciliation 
proceedings and in connection with the strike notice. His 


evidence appears to be highly interested, Kalachand Gope 
admits that he was appointed as a General Mazdoor but says 
that he was asked to carry motors, cables, gear boxes and 
chains etc. from the surface to underground and also to 
places of work in underground. In cross-examination, he 
admitted that there are separate employees for shaft-sinking 
and It is these ermrloyccs who cany goods from surface 
to underground. He went on to state that he and the other 
eight are fitters, and the duties of a fitter mazdoor is to 
carry tools used by fittcis, while fitter-helpers help fitters 
m the actual job of fitting. Shridher Chandra Roy has deposed 
that these nine were engaged for transporting pans, gear 
boxes and other machineries used underground. He has 
further slated that he personally asked them to work on the 
said jobs. This does not appear to be correct becau^ pans 
and gear boxes are not used in the Electrical Engineering 
Department. 1 am of the view that these nine workmen 
have magnified the nature of their duties in order to get the 
wages of Category IV workmen instead of the wages of 
Category 1 or 11 workmen. They would be interested in 
getting a high jump in the wages and may not be willing 
to tell the truth. WW-1 and WW-3 are thick and thin with 
them and aie likely to fall in line with them, MW-1 
K. Bagchi is an Independent person and responsible ofiicer 
and is expected to tell the truth. 1 will, therefore, prefer 
to place reliance upon him, subject to his corroboration by 
documentary evidence. 

10. Ext. M-26 to M-34 arc the service records of these 
nine workmen. Ext, M-26 shows that Bindeswar Gope was 
appointed on .April 8, 1968 ; E.xt. M-27 shows that Sukhdeo 
Gope was appointed on June 9, 1969 ; Ext. M-28 shows 
that Chhabinath Mahato was also appointed on June 9, 1969; 
Ext. M-29 shows that Aklu Mahato was appointed on August 
17 1970; Ext. M-30 shows that Bhim Gorai was appointed 
on July 19, 1971; Ext, M-31 shows that Yudhisthir Karmakar 
was also appointed on July 19, 1971; Ext. M-32 shows that 
Fantush Mahato was also appointed on July 19, 1971; Ext. 
M-33 shows that Kalachand Cope was appointed on June 21, 
1971; and Ext. M-34 shows that Jadu Gope was also ap- 
pointed on June 21, 1971. They were appointed in the 
shaft-sinking department, Their work was on the surface of 
the mine, with the exception of Yudhisthir Karmakar and 
I'antush Mahato whose place of work was underground. 
They were all appointed as miscellaneous mazdoors in Cate- 
gory I. Bindeswar Gope, Sukhdeo Gope and Chhabinath 
were promoted as Category IT workmen with effect from 
January 1, 1974 but the remaining six continue as Category I 
woikmen. Admittedly, therefore, at least three of them are 
no longer Category J workmen and are, indeed. Category If 
workmen, i,e„ instead of being fitters under item no. 3 
of Categoiy I in Appendix V, they have become fitter-helpers 
under item no. 22 of Categoiy H in Appendix V. Exts. M-35 
to M-42 are weekly wage-sheets for the week ending January 
19, 1974, May 11, 1974, May 25, 1974, October 26, 1974, 
November 23. 1974, January 18, 1975 and March 11, 1975 
respectively. These wage-sheets show that in addition to 
Bindeswar Gope, Sukhdeo Gope and Chhabinath Mahato, 
there is one more who was working as a fitter-helper, and 
he is Akin Mahato. He should also have been treated, 
therefore, as a fitter-helper. Category TI and not as a fitter 
Category I. It is true that Ext. M-35 shows that Chhabinath 
received the wages of a Tyndal, Category IV but that, to 
ray mind, will not make any difference in his status as a 
fitter-helper because he received the wages of a Tyndal only 
for three days and that may have been so because he might 
have worked as a Tyndal on these three dates. That, how- 
ever, will not confer a right on him to be categorised as 
a Tvndal Category TV and paid the wages of a Category IV 
workman. 

11. With regard to the first dispute, therefore, my finding 
is that none of these nin-' workmen is a Tyndal and conse- 
quently none of them is entitled to be categorised as a Tyndal, 
Category TV and that Bindeswar Gope, Sukhdeo Gope, 
Chhabinath Mahato and Akin Mahato are not fitters. Cate- 
gory T but fitter-helpers. Category TI from January 1, 1974 
and like the other three Aklu Mahato is al.so entitled to 
be paid wages of Category TT from January 1, 1974. 

12 I will now l.ike tip the second dispute, 

13, Before the nationalisation of non-coking and coking 
coal mines; there were hundreds of such mines in the 
private sector commonly known as market collieries, and 
in addition to them, there were 11 State Collieries.

Eight such State Collieries were in Bihar, two in Onssa 
and one m Madhva Pradt-sh They were owned by the Govern- 
ment of India, Ministry of Production and iipto May 31, 
1944 weic maniRcd by the Railway Board Hence, all 
those pel sons who wcic in service in the Slate Collieiies 
on May 31, 1944 weie governed by the Railway Seivice 

Rules and were allowed the benefits of the Railway Provident 
Fund, Giatuity or special Piovident Fund spcciil cheap 
foodgrains, rent free houses, free electricity, free Railway passes 
and PTOs From June 1, 1944 the management of the 

Stale Colhciics was tiansfeiied to the Depaitment of Supply 
and subsequently to the Ministiy of Production All em 
ployees who cntcicd seivice on or after June 1, 1944 were 

governed by the Civil Rules applicable to all ofhei civil 
employees of the Central Government The Civil employees 
did not get the benefits which the pre-first June, 1944 em- 
ployee were entitled to undei the Railway Service Rules 
but weie entitled to all the conditions of service which were 
applicable to siinilarl:f situated staff in the Central Govein-

on its foimation on October 1, 1956 The N C D C inherited

The pay structuie and other service conditions of the officers 
and the monthly-rated staff continued to be governed by 
the Railway Seivice Rules or the Civil Rules, depending upon 
the fact whether they weic pre-first June, 1944 employees or 
poBt-flrst-June 1944 employees They had ceased to be 
Government sei vants on their absorption by he N C D C 
but their conditions of service including pensionaiy benefits,

N C D C from time to tune in accordance with the recom 
mendafions of the Central Pay Commission 1949 or the 
Second Central Pay Commission, 1959, to the extent 
that these recommendations were accepted by the Govern 
ment of India As and when the necessity for fresh ap 
pomtments arose in lespect of any of the aforesaid cate- 
gories, the NCDC appointed officers monthly-iated

can be termed as Corporation employees The NCDC

per cent of their Basic Pay into the fund and (he NCDC 
had also to contribute to the same extent It also introduced 
a Gratuity Scheme for the newly appointed monthly-rated 
staff The recommendations of the Central Coal Wage Board

1967 The Board lecommcnded that the monhtly paid staff 
should be given fheir choice of cither being on their existmg 
pay-scales which were generally m conformity with the

and other benefits recommended by the Board for the industry

Board recommended that the monthly paid staff of NCDC 
who were then governed by the Central P.ay Commission s 
scale of pay Railway Service Rules Corporation Rules 
or any other rules should be allowed to opt for the scales of 
pay and other service conditions recommended by it foi

Such an option was accordingly given and practically all of

the staff for lack of amenities such as, housing, schools, 
markets, dispensaries etc. Where reasonable amenities already 
exist, there would be no justification for the grant of the 
allowance. The allowance will not be admissible to staff re-

casual labour and staff paid from contingency. Hie allowance

sanctioned originally for such staff shall be reduced in stages

finally. ,\s an exception, in the case of temporary construc-

on a regular basis, the allowance may be continued on a

within the radius of 5 miles of the pioject area and who have 
been recruited locally. See Ext. M-9. There are some

project allowance was admissible to persons reciniited locally. 
Ext. M-17 contains the minutes of the meeting of the Board 


of Directors held on February 15, 1962 and shows that it 
was decided that no project allowance will be admissible 
to persons recruited locally. Ex, M-19 dated March 2, 1966 
is a letter from the Administrative Officer to the Personnel 
Officer enquiring whether the personnel who were previously 
recruited in a project but were subsequently transferred to a 
far away place and then re-lransferred to the locality where 
they had been recruited, were eligible for the project allowance 
or not. Ex, M-10 is the reply given by the Chief Personnel 
Officer to the Administrative Officer on April 26, 1966 
which says that such of the employees who had been trans- 
ferred to their local places and were residing within 5 miles 
radius of the project area, were not entitled to the project 
allowance even thou^ they might have been in receipt of 
Ihc allowance in their former place of posting. Likewise, 
there arc certain documents which will go to show that 
the proiect allowance was given only to officers and monthly- 
rated stalL Ex. M-17 are the Minutes of the 55th Meeting 
of the Board of Directors, referred to above, which 
indicate that the allowance was given only to officers 
and monihly rated staff. Ex, M-7 is the Office Order No. 10 
(.15)/10/NCID/P. A./60/Pt. 1 dated the 4th March, 1964 
which directs that the project allowance was being tpinted 
to officers and monthly paid staff posted to the Churcha 
Project. Ext. M-14 is another office order of even number 
dated July 10, 1964 maintaining the status que with regard 
to the grant of the allowance to the offlMrs and monthly 
paid staff employed in 31 projects, including the MoonidJh 
Project, spcclfled at serial no. 18. That the Office Memo- 
randum Issued by the Ministry of Finance was acted upon 
by the N.C.D.C. in respect of phased reduction and with- 
drawal of the allowance is also supported by various docu- 
ments. F.xt. M'18 (the note prepared for the 55th Meeting) 
proposed the continuance of the reduced rate in the Korba 
Project and Ext. M-17 is the Minutes of the Meeting confirm- 
ing the proposal. Ext. M-8 contains the scheme for the 
progressive withdrawal of the project allowance. The scheme 
provides for a 50 per cent reduction in the project allowance 
once an etnployee was given a house with water, light and 
internal sanitation. This, in effect, meant that once a pukka 
house was allotted with water, light and internal sanitation, 
the.e was a minimum reduction of 50 per cent of the project 
allowance in respect of the individual to whom the house 
was allotted while other employees who may not have been 
allotted houses would continue to be entitled to the full 
project allowance till such time as they were allotted houses 
with the necessary facilities of water, light and internal sani- 
tation. The scheme further provided tnat a further 25 per 
cent 1 eduction would be effected on completion of hospitals, 
schools and markets. This meant that once these amenitiei 
were piovided, those already given houses will have a further 
leducion of 25 per cent in the project allowance, while 
those who may not have been allotted houses would, in any 
case, have to accept a reduction of 25 per cent. The scheme 
further provides that once filtered water supply, and Workers 
Institutes and hospitals in terms of the rules of the Coal 
Mines Welfare Commissioner are provided, the project 
allowance will be fully withdrawn and would be applicable 
to all employees even if some of the emplOTees have not 
occupied Corooration quarters. Ext. M-6 Office Order No. 
13, 1964 defines “house"^ "hospital”, "school” and "filtered 
water”. It further mentions that the reduction of 25 per 
cent with regard to filtered water. Workers Institute and 
hospital, will comprise 15 per cent for the provision of the 
amenity of filtered water supply and 10 per cent for Workers 
Institute and hospital, but no cut of 10 per cent will be made 
where only one of these two amenities, namely. Workers 
Institute or hospital is provided. Ext. M-lt dated December 
18, 1964 shows that where house and filtered water were 
supplied the project allowance was reduced by 65 per cent. 
Ext. M-16 dated June 10, 1966 also shows the same facts. 
Ext, M-12 Is not relevant as it deals with amenities or lack 
of them not in project areas but in regular collieries.

whose Iand.s have been acquited for the project. Officers 
and monthly-rated staff are generally truusferied to the 
Project site from other collieries. The Project Allowance 
was sanctioned by the N.C.D.C. on the pattern of such 
allowance admissible to Central Government employees pos- 
ted in new project areas in order to give relief to them for 
the absence of housing, medical, marketing, schooling and 
other facilities. Those members of the staff who were gover- 
ned by the Railway Service Rules or the Civil Rules were 
automatically given the allowance because of the protection 
given to their previous service conditions. The monthly- 
rated staff was given the allowance at 20 per cent of their 

F ay as none of the facilitie.s existed than in the project area, 

.ater on, the quantum was reduced when some facilities be- 
came available. They were getting the allowance at 7 per 
cent of their basic pay at the time of his deposition. He 
further stated that when the management of the project was 
taken over by the B.C.C.L. in the year 1973 and when its 

ownership also passed to it in the year 1975, the B.C.C.L,

but the allowance was not given to such staff. Ho farther

reside in neighbouring villages within a radius of 5 miles. 
The evidence given by Shridhar Chandra Roy, Kala Chanel 
Gope and S. N, Singh WW-3, WW-2 and WW-I is not 
different.

22. The question is whether there is any justification for 
denying the allowance to the excluded categories. Item No. I 
(iv) of the O.M. Ext.-20 dated January 17, 1975 specifically 
says that the allowance wUl be admissible only to such staff 
as are etnployed on the project and reside within the project 
area or in a nearby locality. The term “nearby locality” 
connotes a radius 5 miles, Tliat being ,o, each employee 

4 I GI/77^ 


who is employed on the project and resides within the pro- 
ject area, demarcate for the purpose, or within a radius of 
3 miles, automatically becomes eligible for grant of the 
allowance. All the workmen employed on the project will 
thus become eligible because it is not the case of the mana- 
gement that any of them is not employed on the project or 
docs not reside within the project area or within a radius 
of .5 miles. It has, therefore, to be seen if inspite of this, 
they fall within the ambit of item No. Kiiij which says that 
the allowance will not be admissible to staff recruited on 
ad-hoc scale of pay, such us daily rated or causal labour 
and staff paid fiom contingencies. The exclusion, therefore, 
depends upon the fact that an employee must be one who 
was appointed on an ad-hoc scale of pay. Now, there is 
nothing to show what is an aJ-hoc sacle of pay but let me 
assume that it is a tentative or a provisional scale and not a 
pre-determined or fixed scale. The workmen are governed 
by the Coal Wage Board rccommenda'ions and their cate- 
gories and wages arc fixed and, therefore, it cannot be said 
ihat they weie recruited on ad-hre scales of pav. There 
thus seems to be no justification for excluding them on tb« 
basis of Ext. M-20. However, Ext. M-20 fixed the project 
allowance for Government projects and not for Corporation,/ 
Company projects, though a choice was given to Corpora- 
tiotv/Company to adopt the project allowance. The N.C.D.C. 
adopted, and in doing so, denied it to local recruits who 
were appointed on ad-hoc scales of pay. A local recruit is 
an original inhabitant of a village within a radius of 5 miles 
of the project area. It follows that if there are workmen 
who are original inhabitants of villages within a radius of 
5 miles of the project area, they would be local recruits ; 
and if, on the other hand, they arc orijjinal inhabitants of 
villages outside the limit of 5 miles 'radius, they will not be 
local recruits and they will become eligible for the project 
allowance. The evidence shows that the strength of the 
monthly-rated staff in the Moonidih Project on April 1, 1974 
was 286 which swelled to 326 on April I, 1975 and to 

380 on April 1, 1976. The stiength of daily-rated and 
piece-rated workmen was 1300 on April 1, 1974 and it rose 
to 1800 on April 1, 1975 and to 2300 on April 1, 1976. 
See the evidence of MW-3 Hari Das. No evidence has been 
led on behalf of the management to show a.s to how many 
of the monthly-rated staff and as to how many of the 

daily and piece-rated workmen are local recruits and how 
many outsiders. The allowance can be denied only to local 

reciuits and not to outsiders. It was the duty of the 

management to have given the break up. Ext. M-23 which 
gives the total man-power strength ,and not the break np 
is thus irrelevant. Sreedhar Chandra Roy W.W. 3 stated 
that 25 per cent of the man-power is locally jecruited and 
75 per cent are outsiders. W.W 2 Kalachand Gope has 
stated th.it the majority came from V.vransi, Arrah, Oiridih, 
and even fiom the States of West Bengal and Mad'as. These 
outsiders cannot be excluded from the grant of the piojcct 
allowance. Besides, the grant of the allowance (o one cate- 
gory of monthly-rated staff and to deny it to another cate- 
eoiv of the same kind of staff, will introduc-c an insidious 
distinction and cause heart-hiirning and industi-ial unrest. 
It is true that the management can lay down the rolicy to 
clve it 'o one category and deny it to another but the classi- 
fication must be rational and intelligible and not arbitrary, 
ft denied the nroiect allovvancc to local recruits and not 
to outsiders even though the hitter be daiFy-rateis .and piece- 
raters. T ikewise, if if grants Ihe allowance to monthlv-rater'., 
il cannot be allowed to pick and choose and say that i* 
would civ'e il to one croup and not to a se.ord group, al- 
though both the groups aie pwintbly-raters

reasons why the project allowance is not beinc given to some 
categories indicated above. The first ground given is that 
the monthly-raters trunsferred or appointed bv the B.C.C.T , 
for tlici Moonidih Proiect were not governed by the scheme 
of pioicct allowance governing the old staff and, therefore,

Hi's second ground is that the nanagement ha p'nt the finan-

in 1975-76. He further deposed that the project incurred 
a loss of Rs. 2.73 crores in 1973-74; Rs, 2.85 croies in 
1974-75 and Rs. 3.17 crores in 1975-76. All capital pro- 
jects require a heavy investment, which starts yielding profits 
after some years when production starls in full measure. 
Besides, if the project is prepared to incur loss and yet pay 
the allowance to some categories, it could as well pay to 
other categories also, or abrogate the allowance for all and 
not retain it for some and deny it to others. Again, the 
figures worked out Include the figures for officers and 
monthly-rated staff to whom the allowance is already being 
given and these figures should have been excluded to find 
Qiit the additional expenditure involved if the allowance is 
paid to those who are at present excluded. Another ground 
taken is that the daily and piece-rated workmen are entitled 
to the benefits of C.M.P.F. Scheme but the officers and 
monthly-rated staff are dcbaiTed from joining that scheme 
and thus if they lose in one matter, they gain in another. 
This ground is correct in part only. Clause (g) of para 2 
of the C.M.P.F. Scheme excludes the old employees of the 
N.C.D.C. from the benefits of the Scheme. The daily and 
piece raters are, however, not excluded because they are 
governed by Coal Wage Board pay scales. They contribute 
at the rate of 8 per cent of their total wage-packet : Basic, 
D.A., V.D.A., U.G.A., O.T. etc— and the N.C.D.C. also 
makes a matching contribution. The N.C.D.C. has, however, 
its own separate P.F. Scheme for its officers and monthly- 
raters who contribute 8 ’A per cent of their ba.sic wages 
only and the N.C.D.C. makes a matching contribution of 
its own. This is a very minor difference which can bo 
ignored. Another ground taken is that whereas daUy-raters 
and piece-raters have to pay a rent of Rs. 2 per month only 
for their quarters, an officer and a monthly-rater has to pay 
10 per cent of his saiary or the standard rent whichever is less 
and here also the daily and piece raters stand to gain. This 
^ound has no substance whatsoever. The project allowance 
is given, inter alia, for the absence of housing facilities. 
Officers and monthly-raters, like daily-raters and piccc-rafere 
have not to pay any house rent, because there are no houses 
in the project area and if and when a house is provided, 
both have to pay, and the former gets u redticed allowance.

24. My award on the first dispute is that Bindeswari Gope, 
Sukhdeo Gope, Akiu Mahato, Chhabinath Mahato, Kalachand 
Ffiotush Mahato, Yudhlstir Karmakar 
and Bhim Gorain are not Tyndal Mlstries and are thus not 
entitled to wages of a Category IV workman. However. 
Bindcs^ri Gope, Sukhdeo Gope, Chhabinath Mahato and 
Akiu Mahato are Fitter-helpers, Category II and not Fittore 
Category I, Bindeswari Gope, Sukhdeo Gope and Chhabi’ 
nath Mahato have been accepted by the management as 
Category H workmen. Akin Mahato is not being treated 
as Category H workman. He will bo so treated and paid 
the wages of Category II from January 1, 1974 since which 
date he is working as a Fitter-helper. My award on the 
second dispute is that the project allowance will not bo paid 
to such of the daily raters and piece-raters as are original 
inhabitants of villages within a radius of 5 miles of the 
project area, It will, however, be paid to such daity-raters 
“Ot original inhabitants of villages 
within the radms of 5 miles of the project area, The raanage- 
ment will work out the two categories to fix the persons to 
whom it will be paid and to whom it will not be paid. In 
order to ensure that the management is not saddled with a 
heavy financial commitment in respect of the previous years 
the award m respect of the project allowance 
shall be enforceable from February 3, 1975 when the rofe 
rence was made.

SO. 2136. — Wheieas (ho Ceniial tiov.uimcru is s dislied 
that the employees of the Oie Handling Plant Site Workshop, 
\ isakhapatnam Port Trust Visakhapatnam, arc otherwise m 
iLspLCt of benefits substantially similar to the benelils provi- 
ded under tlie 1 mployees Stale Insutancc Act, 1948 (34 of 
1948) , 



2334 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JUNE 25. 1977/ASADHA 4, 1899 [Part II— Sec. 3 (ii)] 


Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred hy 
section 90 of the said Act, the Central Oovernment, after 
consultation with the Employees' State Insurance Corporation, 
hereby exempts the abovementioned factoiy from the opera* 
tion of the said Act for the period from Ist August, 1969 upto 
and inclusive of the 24th September, 1976. 

2. The above exemption is subject to the following condi- 
tions, namely ; — 

(1) The employer of the said factory shall submit in res- 
pect of the period during which that factory was sub- 
ject to the operation of the said Act (hereinafter re- 
feried to as the said period), such returns in such 
form and containing such particulars as were due 
from it in respect of the said period under the Em- 
ployees’ State Insurance (General) Regulations, 
1950 ; 

(2) Any Inspector tippoinled by the Corporation under 
sub-sectiofi (1) of section 45 of the said Act, or 
other QBicial of the Corporation authorised in this 
behalf shall, for the purposes of — 

(i) verifying the particulars contained in any return 
submitted under sub-section (1) of section 44 for 
the said pei iod ; or 

(ii) ascertaining whether registers and records were 
maintained as required by the Employees’ State 
Insurance (General) Regulations, 19.50 for the 
said period ; or 

(iir) a.sccrtaining whether the employees continue to 
be entitled to benefits provided by the employer 
in cash and kind being benefits in consideration of 
which exemption is being granted under this noti- 
fication ; or 

(iv) ascertaining whether any of the provisions of the 
Act had been complied with during the period 
when such provisions were in force in relation 
to the said factory; 

be empowered to- 
la) require the principal or immediate employer to fur- 
nish to him such information as he may consider 
necessary ; or 

(b) enter any factoiy, establishment, office or other 
premises occupied by such principal or immediate 
employer at any reasonable time and require any 

Cl son found Inchargc thereof to produce to such 
nspector or other official and allow him to 
examine such accounts, books and other documents 
relating to the employment of persons and pay- 
ment of wages or to furnish to him such infoima- 
tion as he may consider necessary ; or 

(c) examine the principal or immediate employer, his 
agent or servant, or any person found in such fac- 
tory, establishment, oiilce o)' other premises, or 
any person whom the said Inspector or other 
officii has reasonable cause to believe to have 
been an employee ; or 

(d) make copies of or lake extracts from, any register, 
account book or other document maintained in 
such factory, establishment, office or other pre- 
mises. 

EXPLANATORY MEMORANDUM 

It has become ncccss,iry to give retrospective effect to 
the exemption in this case, as the request from the employer 
for the grant of exemption was received late. However, it is 
certified that the factory is eligible for exemption. It is also 
certified (hat the grant of exemption with retrospective effect 
will not affect the interest of anybody adveisely.

New Delhi, the 6th June, 1977 

S.O. 2137.— In exercise of the powers conferred by sec- 
tion 88 of the Employees’ State Insurance Act, 1948 (34 of 
1948) and in continuation of the notification of the Govern- 
ment of India in the Ministry of Labour No. S.O. 1025 
dated the 26th March, 1973 the Central Government hereby 
exempts the regular employees of the Central Workshop, 
Small Industries Service Institute (Ministry of Industrial De- 
velopment, Govenunent of India), Guindy, from the operation 
of the said Act for a further period of 3 years with effect 
from the 7th April, 1974 upto and inclusive of the 6th April, 
1977, 

2. The above exemption is subject to the following condi- 
tions, namely ; — 

(1) The aforesaid factory wherein the employees are em- 
ployed shall maintain a register showing the names 
and designations of the exempted employees ; 

(2) Notwithstanding this exemption, the employees shall 
continue to receive such benefits under the said Act 
to which they might have become entitled to on the 
basis of the contributions paid prior to the date 
from which exemption granted by this notification 
operates; 

(3) The contributions for the exempted period, if alre.idy 
paid, shall not be refunded ; 

(4) The employer of the said factory shall submit in 
respect of t!he period during which that factoiy was 
subject to the operation of the said Act (hereinafter 
referred to as the said period), such returns in such 
form and containing such particulars as were due 
from it in respect of the said periotl under the Fm- 
ployecs’ State Insurance (General) Regulations, 
1950 ; 

(5) Any Inspector appointed by the Corporation under 
sub-section (11 of section 45 of the said Act, or 
other Official of the Corporation authorised in this 
behalf shall, for the pm poses of. — 

(i) verifying the particulars contained in any return 
submitted under sub-section (1) of section 44 for 
the said period; or 

(li) ascertaining whether registers and records were 
maintained us required by the Employees’ State 


Insurance (General) Regulations, 1950 for the 
said period ; or 

(iii) ascertaining whcthci the employees continue to be 
entitled to benefits provided by the employer in 
cash and kind being benefits m consideration of 
which exemption is being granted under this noti- 
fication ; or 

(iv) ascertaining whethei any of the provisions of the 
Act had been complied with during the period when 
such provisions were in force in relation to the 
said factory; 

be empowered to — 

(a) require the principal or immediate employer to 
furnish to him such information as he may consi- 
der necessary ; or 

(b) enter any factory, establishment, office or other 
premises occupied by such principal or immediate 
employer at any reasonable time and require any 
person found incharge thereof to produce to such 
Inspector or other official and allow him to exa- 
mine such accounts, books and other documents 
relating to the employment of persons and pay- 
rnent of wages or to furnish to him such informa- 
tion as he may consider necessary ; or 

(c) examine the principal or immediate employer, hJsi 
agent or servant, or any person found in such 
factory, estabfishment, office or other premises, 
or any person whom the said Inspector or other 
official has reasonable cause to believe to have 
been employee ; or 

(d) make copies of or take extracts from, any register, 
account book or other document maintained in 
such factory, establishment, office or other pre- 
mises. 

EXPLANATORY MEMORANDUM 

It has become necessary to give retrospective effect to the 
exemption in this case, as the recommendation of the Director 
General, Employees’ State Insurance Corporation for the grant 
of exemption was recrived late, However, it is certified that 
the conditions under which the repilar employees of the 
factory were initially granted exemption still persist and they 
are eligible for exemption. It is also certified that the grant 
of exemption with retrasncctive effect will not affect the in- 
terest of anybody adversely.

S.O. 2138, — In exercise of the powers confened by section 
87 of the tmployees’ Stale Insurtince Act, 1948 (34 of 1948) 
and in eontinuntion of the nolilication of the Government of 
India in the Ministiy of J nboiir No, S. O, 2047 dated the

the Central Scientific instruments Organisaion, Chandigarh, 
from the opeiation of the s.iid Act for a further period with 
effect from the 15th June, 1976 upto and inclusive of the 
30th Apiil, 1977. 

2. The above exemption is subject to the following con- 
ditions, namely ; — 


(1) The employer of the said factory shall submit in respect 
of the period during which that factory was subject to the 
operation of the said Act (hereinafter referred to as the said 
period!, such returns in such form and containing such partl- 
ciiliirs us were due tiom it in respect of the s.iid period under 
the Employees’ State Insuiance (General) Regulation 1950; 

(2) ,\iiy Inspector appointed by the Corporation under sub- 
section (1) of section 45 of the said Act, or other Otficial 
()l the t oipoiution authorised in this behalf shall, for the 
purposes of— 

(i) verifying the particulars contained in any return sub- 

mitted under sub-section tl) of section 44 for the 
said period ; or 

(ii) ascertaining whether registers and records were main- 

tained as required by the Employees’ State Insurance 
(General) Regulations, 1950 for the said period : or 

(iii) ascertaining whether the employees continue to be 

entitled to benefits provided hy the emfiloyer in cash 
and kind being henefils in consideration of which 
exemption is being granted under this notification ; 
or 

(is) ascertaining whcthei any of the piovisions of the Act 
has been complied with dining the period when 
such provisions were in force in relation to the said 
factory ; 

be empowered to— 

(a) require the piincipal or immediate employer to furnish 

to him such infoimation ns he may consider 
necessary ; or 

(b) enter any factory, establishment, ofllce or other 

premises occupied by such principal or immediate 
employer at any reasonable lime and require any 
person found inchai^c thereof lo produce to such 
Insnecior or other official and allow him to examine 
such accounts, books and other documents relating 
to the employment of persons and payment of wages

consider necessary ; oi 

(c) examine the principal or immediate employer, hN 

agent or servant, or any peison found in such fac- 
tory, establishment, office or other premises, or any

reasonable cause lo believe lo have been an em- 
ployee ; or 

(d) make copies of or take extracts from any register, 

account book or other document maintained in such 
factory, establishment, office or other premises. 

EXPLANATORY MEMORANDUM

New Delhi, the 10th June, 1977 

S.O. 2140 — ^Whereas it appeals to the Central Government 
that the employer and the majority of the employees in 
relation to the establishment known as Messra 
Bharat Aluminium Udyog, 10/.1, Mall Road, Calcutta-28, have 
agreed that the provisions of the Employees’ Provident Funds 
and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952 (19 of 1952), should 
be made applicable to the said establishment ; 

Now, therefore, In exercise of the powers conferred by sub- 
section (4) of section 1 of the said Act. the Central Govern- 
ment hereby applies the provisions of the said Act to the said 
establishment.

ORDER 

New Delhi, the I3th May, 1977 

S.O. 2169. — Whereas the industrial disputes specified 
ill the Schedule hereto annexed are pending before the Presiding 
Officer, Central Government Industrial Tribunal, Delhi; 

And whereas for administiativc reasons, the Central 
Government considers it desirable to transfer the said disputes 
for adjudication to the Central Government Industrial Tribunal, 
Now Delhi; 



['iTHT II — VI? 3(ii)] 


2347 


liin:? ??i xjirrer : 25 , i 977 /<i?t? 4, 1899 


Now therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred 
by sub-section (,1) of section 33B of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government hereby withdraws 
the proceedings in reiation to the said dispute from the Presi- 
ding Officer, Central Government Industrial Tribunal, Delhi 
and transfers the same to the Central Government Industrial 
Tribunal, New Delhi constituted under section 7A of the said 
Act and directs that the said Central Government Industrial 
Tribunal, New Delhi, shall proceed with the said proceedings 
from the stage at which they are transferred to it and dispose 
of the same according to law.

S.O. 2170. — In exercise of the powers conferred by rule 31 
of the Iron Ore Mines Labour Welfare Cess Rules, 1963, 
the Central Government hereby specifies the Welfare 
Commissioner, Iron Ore Mines Labour Welfare Orga- 
nisation in the State of Madhya Pradesh to be Iron Ore Mines 
Cess Commissioner who shall be exclusively responsible in the 
territories within his jurisdiction foi the assessment and collec- 
tion of the duty of excise levied under clause (b) of section 
2 of the Iron Ore Mines Labour Welfare Cess Act, 1961 (58 
of 1961) and accrued due before the 1st day of October, 
1974.
